380 — Video Codec Basics (H.264-style: motion est. + DCT + entropy) from Scratch

Project Number: 380

Project Name: Video Codec Basics (H.264-style: motion est. + DCT + entropy) from Scratch

Owner: Mujir Qureshi

Document: FINAL_BLUEPRINT.md

Status: specification — locked

1. Goal

1.1 Precise Engineering Scope

Build a self-contained simplified H.264/AVC-style video codec in Rust: an encoder and a decoder that share bitstream format, reconstruction loop, and entropy tables. The system covers YUV 4:2:0 frame handling, macroblock-partitioned intra/inter prediction, integer DCT with quantization, CAVLC and simplified CABAC entropy coding, in-loop deblocking, and an I/P/B GOP structure carried in a NAL-unit bitstream. Bit-exact decode is required: two conformant decoder builds on different machines produce identical output pixels from the same bitstream.

1.2 System Boundaries

Boundary Inside Outside
Input Raw YUV 4:2:0 planar files; RGB input via vc-color Container formats (MP4, MKV, TS)
Output Raw YUV 4:2:0 planar files; NAL-unit bytestream Muxed containers
Profiles Simplified Baseline/Main intra + inter + B High/Hi10/Hi444 extended profiles
Entropy CAVLC + simplified CABAC Full CABAC context model tables (AVC Annex D)
Rate control CBR-approximation via QP stepping Two-pass VBR, scene-cut lookahead
Intra modes 4x4 (9 modes) + 16x16 (4 modes) + chroma 8x8 8x8 luma intra (profile High)
Threading Single-threaded encoder + decoder Slice-parallel, wavefront parallel
Hardware Software-only GPU/DSP offload
Conformance Self-conformant (encoder decode matches decoder) ITU-T H.264 4:4:4/SVC/MVC

1.3 Non-Goals

  • No real-time streaming stack (RTP/RTSP/HLS); codec only.
  • No hardware-accelerated path (NVENC, VideoToolbox, VAAPI).
  • No adaptive streaming ABR ladder.
  • No weighted prediction beyond simple average.
  • No SEI/VUI metadata beyond minimal PTS signaling.
  • No error concealment for packet-loss recovery.
  • No audio codec; video codec only.
  • No full AVC Annex B compliance beyond the tested subset.

1.4 Intended Deployment Model

  • CLI tool (vc-cli) invoked from shell: encode a YUV file to a NAL bytestream, decode a bytestream to YUV, measure PSNR, inspect bitstream structure.
  • Library crates consumed by downstream projects (other portfolio codecs, test harnesses).
  • Designed for single-host, single-process, single-thread execution; no server, no daemon, no network exposure.

1.5 Target Users / Operators / Developers

Persona Use
Portfolio engineer Study H.264 mechanics end-to-end in a single codebase
Systems curriculum Teach DCT, entropy coding, motion search as isolated, testable crates
Codec researcher Experiment with mode decision, rate control, entropy tables without licensing constraints
Test-harness author Property-based roundtrip tests against known-good bitstreams

1.6 Phase 0 Deliverable — Locked Crates

The following crates are defined at project initialization; their public API signatures are locked before coding begins:

  1. vc-core — Plane, Frame, FrameType, Macroblock, MbType, Partition, MotionVector, IntraMode, TransformBlock, Qp, NalUnit, SliceHeader, RefList, Dpb; no I/O, no codec logic.
  2. vc-color — RGB↔YUV BT.601/BT.709 conversion; 4:2:0 downsample/upsample; plane allocation + stride management.
  3. vc-transform — integer 4x4 / 8x8 DCT forward/inverse; Hadamard DC; bit-exact inversion.
  4. vc-quant — QP ladder (0–51), multiply-shift forward quant + dequant, dead-zone, chroma QP mapping.
  5. vc-intra — 4x4 luma (9 directional modes), 16x16 luma (4 modes), 8x8 chroma; causal neighbor availability.
  6. vc-motion — full-search + diamond/EPZS block matching; SAD/SATD cost; RD-weighted lambda cost.
  7. vc-inter — half-pel 6-tap interpolation, quarter-pel bilinear; MC block fetch; MV prediction; chroma 1/8-pel.
  8. vc-entropy — CAVLC (coeff_token, trailing-ones, levels, total_zeros, run_before VLC tables); simplified CABAC (binary arithmetic coder, context models, renormalization, bypass).
  9. vc-deblock — boundary-strength computation; luma + chroma edge filtering; QP-derived alpha/beta thresholds.
  10. vc-bitstream — MSB-first BitWriter/BitReader; unsigned/signed exp-Golomb (ue/se); NAL unit framing; emulation-prevention byte stuffing (0x000003); RBSP trailing bits.
  11. vc-encoder — encode driver: GOP scheduling, RD mode decision, basic QP-stepping rate control, reconstruction loop (must match decoder output pixel-for-pixel).
  12. vc-decoder — decode driver: NAL parse → entropy decode → dequant → inverse DCT → prediction → deblocking → DPB management.
  13. vc-cli — encode | decode | psnr | inspect subcommands; argument parsing; YUV I/O.

1.7 What Later Phases Unlock

Phase range Capability unlocked
Phases 1–2 Frame I/O, bitstream substrate
Phases 3–4 Transform + quantization; lossless roundtrip proofs
Phase 5 Intra prediction; first real compression
Phase 6 First end-to-end I-frame encode/decode + PSNR measurement
Phases 7 CAVLC replaces placeholder coding; conformant entropy
Phases 8–10 P-frame inter prediction; motion search; temporal compression
Phase 11 B-frames, full GOP, DPB reference management
Phase 12 In-loop deblocking filter; reference quality improvement
Phase 13 CABAC, full RD mode decision, rate control
Phase 14 Conformance hardening, fuzz, benchmarks

Total: Phase 0 to full codec ~20–24 weeks

1.10 Language Selection — Rust

A codec is block-based pixel processing on a tight per-macroblock loop: integer DCT, motion search, deblocking, and a bit-precise entropy coder where the decoder must reconstruct bit-exactly and the encoder's reconstruction loop must match it to avoid drift. Rust gives fixed-width integer transforms (bit-exact, no float divergence), no GC on the hot path, exhaustive enums for macroblock/prediction modes, and precise control over the entropy-coder bit buffer; the transforms and motion search are SIMD-amenable later. Encoder, decoder, transforms, prediction, entropy coders, deblocking, and bitstream are all Rust.

1.11 Required Prerequisites

Domain Specific knowledge needed
Linear algebra 2-D separable transforms; butterfly factorizations
Digital signal processing Sampling theory; spatial frequency; filter kernels
Integer arithmetic Fixed-point multiply-shift; overflow management; rounding modes
Information theory Entropy, Huffman codes, arithmetic coding, context modeling
Video fundamentals YUV color space; chroma subsampling; temporal prediction
Rust Bit manipulation; unsafe-free Vec<u8> I/O; property-based testing (proptest)
Systems Cache line effects on pixel loops; SIMD alignment

2. Why Build This

2.1 Unique Systems Knowledge Taught

Topic Mechanism learned
Integer DCT How to factor a floating-point DCT into exact integer butterfly passes without roundoff accumulation
Quantization QP ladder design; multiply-shift dequant; dead-zone effect on sparse coefficients
Intra prediction Spatial prediction from causal decoded neighbors; directional extrapolation; DC/mean fallback
Motion estimation Block-matching search strategies; SAD vs SATD cost; quarter-pel precision through interpolation
Entropy coding CAVLC run/level/coeff coding via lookup tables; binary arithmetic coding renormalization; context selection
Rate-distortion Lambda weighting; mode competition; how compression decisions trade bits against distortion
Reconstruction loop Why the encoder must decode its own output to avoid drift; the feedback path topology
In-loop filtering Why deblocking is inside the coding loop, not post-processing; boundary-strength derivation
DPB management Reference list construction; picture marking; POC reordering; frame buffer reuse
Bitstream design Self-delimiting exp-Golomb coding; emulation-prevention; NAL/RBSP layering

2.2 Historical and Architectural Significance

H.264/AVC (2003) is the dominant delivery codec across streaming, video conferencing, Blu-ray, and mobile; its design decisions (integer DCT, CABAC, in-loop filter, quarter-pel MC) became the baseline for HEVC/H.265, VP9, and AV1. Every modern video codec is an evolution of the H.264 block-transform pipeline. Understanding this design at the source-code level is a prerequisite for evaluating next-generation codecs.

2.3 Engineering Muscles Developed

  • Bit-exact integer arithmetic: designing transforms that close under inverse on all platforms without float.
  • Pipeline thinking: encoder = predict → residual → transform → quant → entropy → bitstream; decoder reverses exactly.
  • Feedback loop correctness: the reconstruction loop is a closed control system; drift is a real production failure mode.
  • Property-based testing at scale: roundtrip invariants (I3, I6, I7) drive property tests, not just unit tests.
  • Performance on bounded memory: codec operates within a per-frame working set; cache pressure is a primary concern.
  • Complexity management: 13 crates with strict dependency ordering; no circular dependencies.

2.4 Production Engineering Concepts Exercised

Concept How exercised
Deterministic output I1: identical pixels from identical bitstream, cross-platform
Feedback loop stability I2: encoder reconstruction loop; drift = unrecoverable quality collapse
Bounded error I5: quant-dequant error bounded by QP; verifiable by property test
Fuzzing critical parsers Phase 14: bitstream parser must reject malformed NALs without panicking
Benchmark-driven optimization Phase 14: per-frame encode/decode throughput; identify hot loops
Observability P10: per-frame PSNR, bits, QP, mode-distribution metrics
Conformance testing Phase 14: encoder reconstruction vs decoder reconstruction comparison

2.5 Cross-Links to Related Portfolio Projects

Project Connection
#173 — JPEG/PNG Image Codec Same DCT + entropy sibling; 2-D block transform, quantization tables, Huffman coding — H.264's intra is JPEG with integer transform + context-adaptive entropy
#174 — Audio DSP Overlap-add filter design; signal-domain thinking; perceptual distortion metrics
#369 — FFT Frequency-domain analysis; separable transform theory; butterfly factorization patterns
#397 — General-Purpose Compression Range coding (CABAC arithmetic coder shares renormalization logic); LZ concepts in motion search
#403 — Video Entropy Stage CABAC range coding is the exact arithmetic coder from that project applied to video coefficients

2.6 Calibration Value

Building this codec places the engineer at the intersection of DSP, compiler-like mode decision, information theory, and systems programming. It requires making each abstraction layer testable in isolation (property tests), then integrating them into a pipeline that must close bit-exactly. An engineer who completes this project can read H.265/HEVC or AV1 specifications and locate the equivalent subsystem within a week. It is the most complete single-project demonstration of signal processing + systems + algorithm integration in the portfolio.

2.7 What Mastery Looks Like

After building this the engineer holds these exact competencies, each tied to a phase:

  • Hybrid block-codec architecture (Phase 1–3): can specify the predict → transform → quantize → entropy pipeline and explain precisely where information is lost (quantization only).
  • The reconstruction loop and drift (Phase 5–6): can explain why the encoder must decode its own output, and debug a single-bit encoder/decoder drift to its source (I2).
  • Integer transform + quantization (Phase 4): can implement a bit-exact H.264 integer butterfly and reason about why floating point is forbidden in the hot path (P1/P3).
  • Motion estimation/compensation (Phase 8–9): can implement diamond/EPZS search with SATD cost, sub-pel interpolation, and explain motion search as a rate-distortion proxy.
  • Entropy coding roundtrip (Phase 7, Phase 13): can implement CAVLC and simplified CABAC and prove encode→decode is identity (I6).
  • In-loop deblocking (Phase 10): can explain why the filter is normative and inside the loop, and what drifts if encoder and decoder filter differently.
  • Rate-distortion mode decision + rate control (Phase 11–12): can derive λ from QP and explain Lagrangian RD optimization vs greedy throughput-first decisions.
  • Conformance + fuzzing discipline (Phase 14): can build a byte-exact golden-file conformance harness and fuzz the bitstream parser to a no-panic guarantee (I7).

3. Architectural Overview

3.1 Canonical Architecture Diagram

                      VIDEO CODEC BASICS  (H.264-style encoder + decoder)
+------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
|   ENCODER                                                                           |
|   raw YUV ---> vc-color ---> per-macroblock:                                         |
|        mode decision (RD): vc-intra  OR  vc-motion -> vc-inter                       |
|              |                                  |                                    |
|              v       prediction                 v                                    |
|        residual = source - prediction                                               |
|              |                                                                       |
|              v                                                                       |
|        vc-transform (int DCT) -> vc-quant -> vc-entropy (CAVLC/CABAC) -> vc-bitstream |
|              |                                                          |  NAL units  |
|              |  RECONSTRUCTION LOOP (must match decoder, drift-free):    v            |
|              +--> dequant -> inverse DCT -> + prediction -> vc-deblock -> DPB         |
|                                                                  (reference frames)   |
|   ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
|   DECODER                                                                            |
|   bitstream ---> vc-bitstream (parse NAL/slice/MB) ---> vc-entropy (decode coeffs+mv) |
|        |                                                       |                      |
|        v                                                       v                      |
|   vc-quant (dequant) -> vc-transform (inverse DCT) = residual                         |
|        |                                                                             |
|        v  + prediction (vc-intra / vc-inter from DPB) -> reconstruct                  |
|        v                                                                             |
|   vc-deblock (in-loop) -> output frame + store in DPB                                 |
+------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+

3.2 Subsystem Descriptions

vc-color (raw YUV → encoder input / decoder output) Converts RGB24 to YUV 4:2:0 via BT.601 or BT.709 matrix; performs 2:1 chroma downsample (luma-aligned) and upsample. Manages Plane allocation with configurable stride for SIMD alignment. Data flows in as raw byte buffers; flows out as Frame{planes: [Plane;3]}. No codec logic; pure color math. Trust boundary: all pixel values are clamped to [0,255] before leaving this crate.

vc-intra (intra prediction) Computes prediction pixels for all 4x4 luma modes (DC, Vertical, Horizontal, Diagonal Down-Left, Diagonal Down-Right, Vertical-Right, Horizontal-Down, Vertical-Left, Horizontal-Up) and 16x16 luma modes (DC, Horizontal, Vertical, Plane), plus 8x8 chroma. Reads only already-decoded neighbor samples (left column, top row) — I4 enforced structurally by requiring a DecodedNeighbors argument. Outputs a prediction block; does not modify the Frame. The encoder calls this speculatively for mode decision; the decoder calls it once, committed.

vc-motion (motion estimation) Consumes a source macroblock and a reference Frame from the DPB; returns the best integer-pel MotionVector and its RD cost. Implements full-search and diamond/EPZS patterns. SAD is the primary distortion measure; SATD (Hadamard-transformed SAD) approximates frequency-weighted distortion. Lambda parameter from vc-encoder weights bit cost against distortion. No pixel modification; pure search. Concurrency: stateless, safe to run per-macroblock without coordination.

vc-inter (motion compensation / sub-pixel interpolation) Takes a MotionVector (quarter-pel precision) and a reference Frame; produces a prediction block. Half-pel positions generated via the H.264 6-tap luma filter (coefficients [1,-5,20,20,-5,1]/32); quarter-pel by bilinear averaging of adjacent half-pel samples. Chroma uses 1/8-pel bilinear. Edge extension (replicate) handles out-of-frame MVs. Outputs a Plane-sized prediction; used by both encoder (reconstruction) and decoder (prediction).

vc-transform (integer DCT) Forward and inverse 4x4 integer DCT derived from the H.264/AVC specification (Cf/Ci matrices, integer butterfly, shift-and-round). 8x8 variant for profile-High macroblocks. Hadamard 4x4 for the 16x16 luma DC block. The critical invariant I3 holds at this layer: inverse_dct(forward_dct(b)) reproduces b exactly within the defined integer precision. No float arithmetic anywhere.

vc-quant (quantization + dequantization) Maps QP ∈ [0,51] to a multiply-shift pair (forward: (coeff * MF + f) >> qbits; inverse: coeff_q * V << qp/6). Dead-zone offsets for intra vs inter block classification. Chroma QP is remapped via the standard 6-entry table. Enforces I5: dequantized output within one step size of original coefficient. Inputs: TransformBlock of integer coefficients + Qp. Outputs: quantized TransformBlock.

vc-entropy (CAVLC + CABAC) Two sub-modules sharing the BitWriter/BitReader from vc-bitstream. CAVLC: encodes/decodes quantized residual via the standard coeff_token (nC-indexed), trailing_ones, levels (suffix_length), total_zeros, run_before tables; also encodes MB type, intra modes, MVDs via exp-Golomb or mapped VLC. CABAC: binary arithmetic coder with context initialization (cabac_init_idc), context-adaptive probability update (MPS/LPS tables), renormalization loop, bypass mode for equiprobable symbols. Invariant I6 enforced by roundtrip tests.

vc-bitstream (NAL / RBSP / bit I/O) BitWriter: MSB-first bit packing into Vec<u8>; emits NAL unit start codes (0x000001) and emulation-prevention bytes (0x000003) in RBSP. BitReader: inverse parsing. Exp-Golomb ue(v)/se(v) encode/decode. Slice header, macroblock header, and partition syntax serialization. Invariant I7: parse(serialize(x)) == x for all valid syntax elements. This crate has no codec logic; it is a pure bitstream I/O substrate.

vc-deblock (in-loop deblocking filter) Computes boundary strength (BS 0–4) for each 4x4 edge from macroblock type, coded-block-pattern, and motion-vector differences. Applies the H.264 adaptive edge filter using QP-derived thresholds alpha/beta/tC0 (luma) and equivalent chroma thresholds. Applied inside the reconstruction loop in both encoder and decoder — not as post-processing — because reference frames must be identical (I9). Inputs: reconstructed Frame, Macroblock array with BS data, QP map. Outputs: filtered Frame in place.

vc-encoder (encode driver) Schedules the GOP (IDR → P → B cadence); calls vc-intra and vc-motion/vc-inter for mode decision; selects the mode minimizing lambda * bits + distortion; calls vc-transform, vc-quant, vc-entropy, vc-bitstream in sequence; runs the reconstruction loop (dequant + inverse DCT + prediction + vc-deblock) after each macroblock; stores reconstructed frames in the DPB. QP-stepping rate control adjusts QP per-frame toward a target bitrate. Emits NAL units. The reconstruction loop is the most critical correctness constraint (I2).

vc-decoder (decode driver) Parses NAL units via vc-bitstream; entropy-decodes coefficients and MVs via vc-entropy; dequantizes via vc-quant; inverse-transforms via vc-transform; applies prediction (vc-intra or vc-inter) using the DPB; runs vc-deblock in-loop; stores output frames in DPB and emits display-order frames. Must produce pixel-identical output to the encoder's reconstruction loop (I1, I2).

vc-cli (command-line driver) Subcommands: encode (YUV file → NAL bytestream, configurable QP/GOP/profile), decode (bytestream → YUV file), psnr (compare two YUV files, report per-component PSNR in dB), inspect (parse and print NAL/slice/macroblock structure without full decode). No codec logic; coordinates vc-encoder or vc-decoder with file I/O. Observability: prints per-frame bits, PSNR, QP, and mode distribution.

DPB (decoded picture buffer, in vc-encoder + vc-decoder) In-memory ring of decoded Frame objects used as motion-compensation references. Manages reference list (RefList) construction: list0 (past frames for P/B), list1 (future frames for B). POC-ordered insertion; sliding-window or explicit MMCO frame marking. Capacity bounded by the configured reference frame count (default 4). Shared between encoder reconstruction loop and inter predictor.

4. System Constraints

4.1 Quantified Engineering Targets

ID Constraint Target Notes
M1 Working memory per frame (1080p) ≤ 128 MB Includes DPB (4 refs × ~6 MB YUV each), transform scratch, entropy tables
— Working memory per frame (720p) ≤ 48 MB Same accounting at lower resolution
— Working memory per frame (480p) ≤ 18 MB
M2 Peak heap (encode, 1080p, 4 refs) ≤ 200 MB Encoder scratch + 4-frame DPB + bitstream buffer
M3 Peak heap (decode, 1080p, 4 refs) ≤ 150 MB Decoder DPB + parse buffers
M4 Encode throughput — target (720p) ≥ 5 fps Single-threaded, QP=28, baseline settings, no SIMD required
M5 Encode throughput — target (480p) ≥ 15 fps Same conditions
M6 Decode throughput — target (720p) ≥ 30 fps Single-threaded; decode is faster than encode
M7 Decode throughput — target (480p) ≥ 60 fps
M8 Per-macroblock encode latency (720p) ≤ 5 ms p99 Includes mode decision + transform + quant + entropy + reconstruct
M9 Per-macroblock decode latency (720p) ≤ 1 ms p99 Inverse pipeline without search
M10 Per-macroblock mode decision (p50) ≤ 500 µs 16×16 macroblock; diamond search + intra check
M11 Motion search time (full, 16×16, ±16) ≤ 2 ms Full search; diamond/EPZS target 10× faster
— Startup / first frame latency ≤ 100 ms Table initialization + first IDR frame encode
— Shutdown / flush ≤ 200 ms Drain B-frame queue; flush DPB
— Concurrency 1 thread (encode), 1 thread (decode) Phase 14 deferred: slice-parallel
M12 Cache footprint — hot macroblock loop ≤ 256 KB Fits in L2 cache on typical x86-64 and ARM64
— Bitstream output buffer ≤ 2× uncompressed frame size NAL byte-stuffing upper bound
— DPB reference frames 1–16 frames configurable Default: 4 for P-frames, 2 for B-reference
— QP range 0–51 (Qp ladder) Maps to H.264 step sizes; QP 0 = lossless-ish
— Max resolution supported 1920×1080 No tiling; single-threaded loop
— Min resolution supported 16×16 One macroblock; unit test baseline
— PSNR correctness floor Y-PSNR ≥ 30 dB at QP=28 for natural content Sanity bound for rate-distortion correctness
— PSNR at QP=18 (high quality) Y-PSNR ≥ 38 dB
— Encoder/decoder drift 0 bits drift across a 300-frame GOP I2: reconstruction loop exact match
— Bitstream parse on malformed input No panic; return Err I7 + fuzz target requirement
— Bitstream parse on valid input 100% success rate No false parse failures
— Encode: storage amplification Bitstream ≤ 3× uncompressed at QP=0 Boundary case; at QP=28 expect 20:1
— Binary size (release, all crates) ≤ 10 MB No embedded large lookup tables beyond the H.264 VLC tables
— Test suite runtime ≤ 60 s All property tests + roundtrip + integration on a 4-core CI host
— Fuzz corpus coverage target ≥ 80% of bitstream parsing paths Measured by llvm-cov

5. Required Reading

Books

# Title / Author / Edition Chapters to Study Concepts to Extract
B1 Richardson, The H.264 Advanced Video Compression Standard, 2nd ed., Wiley 2010 All; focus Ch. 6 (intra), Ch. 7 (inter), Ch. 8 (transform/quant), Ch. 9 (entropy), Ch. 10 (deblocking), Ch. 11 (NAL/profiles) Primary teaching reference; macroblock syntax, integer DCT butterfly constants, CAVLC table derivation, CABAC state machine, deblocking BS tables, NAL framing — applied throughout Phase 3 (transform), Phase 5 (intra), Phase 7 (CAVLC), Phase 12 (deblock), Phase 13 (CABAC)
B2 Richardson, H.264 and MPEG-4 Video Compression, Wiley 2003 Ch. 4 (video coding overview), Ch. 5 (inter prediction), Ch. 6–8 (transform, quant, entropy) Pre-standard framing; useful for understanding which tools H.264 inherited from MPEG-2/H.263 and which are new; applied in §2 (Why Build This) and Phase 10 (inter prediction) design-tradeoff rationale
B3 Rao, Kim, Hwang, Video Coding Standards: AVS, H.264/MPEG-4 AVC, HEVC, VP6, DIRAC, VC-1, Springer 2014 Ch. 3–5 (H.264 section entirely) Comparative treatment of intra/inter/transform/entropy stages across standards; clarifies what is H.264-specific vs generic; applied in the Comparative Architecture Table and Alternative Architectures back-matter sections
B4 Tan, Iain, Digital Video and HD: Algorithms and Interfaces, Morgan Kaufmann 2nd ed. Ch. 3–5 (color spaces, YCbCr), Ch. 8 (GOP structure), Ch. 9–10 (macroblock encoding, DPB management) YUV conversion BT.601/BT.709 formulas; chroma 4:2:0 sampling grid; POC and DPB management patterns for vc-core and vc-color
B5 Sayood, Introduction to Data Compression, 5th ed., Morgan Kaufmann 2017 Ch. 3 (statistical coding, Huffman), Ch. 4 (arithmetic coding), ch. 13 (VLC design) VLC tables underpinning CAVLC; arithmetic coding algorithm underpinning CABAC; probability estimation update rules; context conditioning — theoretical basis for vc-entropy Phase 7 and Phase 13
B6 Bhaskaran, Konstantinidis, Image and Video Compression Standards, Kluwer 1997 Ch. 7 (block matching algorithms), Ch. 8 (sub-pixel interpolation), Ch. 9 (rate control) Full-search vs fast-search block matching; SAD/SATD computational cost; sub-pel interpolation filter design — background for vc-motion Phase 8 and vc-inter Phase 9
B7 Poynton, Digital Video and HD: Algorithms and Interfaces, 2nd ed., Elsevier 2012 Ch. 24–26 (YCbCr matrices, limited/full range), Ch. 28 (chroma siting), Ch. 30 (color difference) BT.601 vs BT.709 matrix coefficients; limited (16–235) vs full (0–255) range; chroma siting (co-sited vs centered) for 4:2:0 — critical for vc-color pixel-exact conversion matching the H.264 spec
B8 Vetterli, Kovacevic, Wavelets and Subband Coding, Prentice Hall 1995 Ch. 5 (DCT as special case of subband decomposition), sec. 5.3–5.5 Mathematical grounding: why the DCT concentrates energy into fewer nonzero coefficients than the DFT; separability; the integer approximation approach and its precision tradeoffs — informs vc-transform butterfly design
B9 Wien, High Efficiency Video Coding: Coding Tools and Specification, Springer 2015 Ch. 1–2 (H.264 as baseline and design limits) HEVC identifies exactly where H.264 is weakest: large CU sizes, fractional interpolation precision, transform flexibility; applied in the Alternative Architectures Alt-D entry (next-generation block codec) and Deferred Features Register scoping
B10 Westerink, Roufs, Subjective Image Quality, IEEE Press 1989 Sections on psychovisual masking, threshold models Perceptual motivation for dead-zone quantization: the human eye tolerates more error near edges and in high-frequency coefficients; directly informs vc-quant dead-zone asymmetry (wider dead-zone for AC coefficients)
B11 Rao, Yip, Discrete Cosine Transform: Algorithms, Advantages, Applications, Academic Press 1990 Ch. 2–3 (DCT-I through DCT-VIII), sec. 6.4 (integer approximations) Complete mathematical treatment of DCT-II (the basis of H.264's transform); factored butterfly forms; integer lifting steps — prerequisite reading before implementing vc-transform

Recommended Reading Order

For a developer starting the project cold, the minimum prerequisite reading before writing code:

  1. B1 Ch. 1–3 — H.264 overview and terminology; establishes vocabulary used throughout this document.
  2. P1 (Wiegand et al. 2003) — Standard overview in 30 pages; understand all tools before implementing any.
  3. S1 sec. 6–8 — Normative syntax and decoding process; read alongside the spec while implementing each phase.
  4. P2 (Malvar et al.) — Read before Phase 3 (DCT) and Phase 4 (quant); contains exact butterfly constants.
  5. P3 (Marpe et al.) — Read before Phase 13 (CABAC); state machine tables are required reading.
  6. P4 (List et al.) — Read before Phase 12 (deblocking); boundary-strength derivation algorithm.
  7. P8 + P12 (Tourapis EPZS) — Read before Phase 8 (motion estimation); candidate list construction.
  8. B5 Ch. 4 (Sayood arithmetic coding) — Read before implementing vc-entropy CABAC in Phase 13; arithmetic interval model.
  9. R1 (JM), R2 (x264) — Consult continuously throughout implementation for validation and tradeoff reference.

Reference Implementations

# Repository Directories / Files to Study Architectural Lessons Pitfalls to Avoid
R1 JM Reference Software (h264reference.sourceforge.net) lencod/src/: encoder.c, image.c (MB loop), intra_prediction.c, me_fullsearch.c, cavlc.c, cabac.c; ldecod/src/: image.c, mb_read.c, deblock.c Ground-truth for every algorithm; compare vc-transform / vc-deblock / vc-entropy outputs against JM on same input; only authoritative validator Research prototype: no separation of concern, global state everywhere, no memory safety; do not mirror file layout or state model in Rust
R2 x264 (code.videolan.org/videolan/x264) encoder/analyse.c (mode decision λ, partition selection), encoder/me.c (EPZS implementation), common/dct.c (butterfly layout), common/quant.c (dead-zone, QP table), encoder/cabac.c (state machine), common/mc.c (quarter-pel filter) Production-quality RD mode decision; EPZS candidate predictor construction; quantization dead-zone tuning; CABAC probability table initialization Do not copy ASM kernels (LGPL + architecture-specific); macro-heavy C style is antithetical to Rust; rate control in ratecontrol.c is more complex than needed here

R8 image-rs/jpeg-decoder (github.com/image-rs/jpeg-decoder) src/idct.rs (integer inverse DCT), src/huffman.rs (Huffman decoder) Idiomatic Rust integer DCT in a production codec (portfolio #173 sibling); shows how to structure fixed-point butterfly as Rust functions with clear type signatures (applied in Phase 3 vc-transform) JPEG DCT uses different constants (cosine weights) than H.264 integer DCT; do not reuse butterfly coefficients; idiomatic Rust structure is safe to borrow

6. Design Principles

P1. Bit-Exact Decoding

Statement. The decoder's reconstruction is fully determined by the bitstream via integer-only arithmetic; two conformant decoders produce identical output pixels.

Rationale. Inter prediction uses reference frames produced by prior decoding. If reconstruction is platform-dependent (float rounding varies by FPU, compiler flags, SIMD lane width), the reference frame used for prediction differs across implementations. Prediction error accumulates multiplicatively across a GOP.

Mechanism. Integer DCT (P3) uses fixed butterfly constants defined in H.264 sec. 8.5. Prediction and deblocking clamp to [0, 255] via saturating u8 arithmetic. No f32/f64 anywhere in vc-transform, vc-intra, vc-inter, vc-deblock reconstruction paths. All shift counts and clamp points are compile-time constants embedded in vc-transform, not computed at runtime from float approximations.

Tradeoff. Integer DCT sacrifices ~0.1–0.2 dB PSNR vs the ideal float DCT. This cost is accepted universally in the H.264 standard. It is amortized by the 3–5 dB PSNR gain from inter prediction over intra-only coding at equivalent bitrates.

Operational implications.

  • CI: decode identical bitstream on x86-64 and aarch64; sha256(output.yuv) must match across both runs.
  • Fuzz: all property tests run under cargo miri to catch platform-dependent behavior and undefined integer operations.
  • Pre-commit hook: grep -rn 'f32\|f64' vc-transform/src vc-intra/src vc-inter/src vc-deblock/src must return no matches.
  • Rate control (vc-encoder) may use f64 for λ computation in mode decision; this is the only permitted float, and it affects only bit-count estimation, not reconstruction pixels.

Violation consequence. Float DCT: encoder on x86 AVX builds different reference frame than decoder on aarch64; inter residuals diverge immediately; PSNR collapses to noise floor within 10–20 P-frames; the codec is undeployable across heterogeneous systems.

Summary table.

Aspect Rule
Crates using integer-only vc-transform, vc-intra, vc-inter, vc-deblock, vc-quant
Float permitted vc-encoder λ computation only
Clamp operation saturating cast to u8, not min(max(x,0),255) in float
Intermediate precision 32-bit signed accumulator; no overflow for 8-bit source input
Cross-platform test sha256(decoded YUV) on x86-64 == sha256(decoded YUV) on aarch64

P2. Drift-Free Encoder Reconstruction Loop

Statement. The encoder predicts from the same reconstructed (dequantized, inverse-transformed, deblocked) frames the decoder will produce — never from the original uncompressed source frames.

Rationale. If the encoder predicts from source but emits quantized residuals, the decoder reconstructs source + quantization_error; the next frame's encoder predicts from source but decoder from source + error₁; by frame N the divergence is Σ errorₙ, unbounded across the GOP.

Mechanism. After CAVLC/CABAC-coding a macroblock, vc-encoder immediately: (1) dequantizes coefficients via vc-quant, (2) inverse-DCTs via vc-transform, (3) adds back the prediction to form recon_mb, (4) stores recon_mb in the encoder-side DPB row buffer. After all MBs in the frame, vc-deblock filters the encoder DPB frame (P6). Future predictions use this encoder DPB, not the source frame buffer.

Tradeoff. Encoder computational cost ≈ encoder-path cost + full decoder-path cost. Macroblock-row parallelism requires careful data-dependency tracking: MB(row=r, col=c) cannot be encoded until MB(r-1, c-1) through MB(r-1, c+1) are fully reconstructed (intra top-row dependency).

Operational implications.

  • vc-encoder ReconLoop struct wraps vc-quant + vc-transform + vc-deblock in identical order to vc-decoder.
  • ReconLoop is not optional; skipping it is a compile error (not a runtime flag).
  • Invariant I2 is a debug_assert_eq!(encoder_dpb_hash, decoder_dpb_hash) after each frame in test builds.
  • Phase 6 introduces the reconstruction loop for I-frames; Phase 10 extends it to P-frames; Phase 11 extends to B-frames.

Violation consequence. Predicting from source: encoder residual ≈ 0 (trivially small); decoder sees large residuals (difference from distorted reference); PSNR collapses after first P-frame boundary; codec is unusable for any GOP longer than one I-frame.

P3. Integer Transforms Only

Statement. Forward and inverse DCT (and Hadamard for 16×16 luma DC) are separable integer butterfly operations with fixed right-shifts at a defined intermediate precision; no floating-point at any step.

Mechanism. The H.264 4×4 integer DCT factors as:

Row pass: butterfly with additions {a+b, a-b, 2a-b, a+2b} — no multiplies
Column pass: same butterfly pattern
Normalization: post-multiply scaling absorbed into vc-quant multiply-shift table
Intermediate precision: 16-bit inputs → 32-bit accumulator (no overflow for 8-bit source)

The Hadamard transform for the 16×16 luma DC layer (vc-intra 16×16 mode) uses the same butterfly structure with all shifts set to zero (exact integer Hadamard). Butterfly constants are compile-time const in vc-transform with a comment citing H.264 sec. 8.5 and Malvar et al. (P2).

Tradeoff. The integer DCT is not lossless without quantization in arbitrary precision: inverse(forward(x)) reproduces x exactly only at the specified 32-bit precision after normalization shift. This is I3 and is tested exhaustively. Precision loss vs ideal float DCT: ~0.1–0.2 dB — uniform across all platforms, which is acceptable.

Operational implications.

  • vc-transform public API: all functions have signatures fn([i16; N]) -> [i32; N], no float parameters anywhere.
  • Exported: forward_dct_4x4, inverse_dct_4x4, forward_dct_8x8, inverse_dct_8x8, hadamard_4x4.
  • Property test (I3): exhaustive inputs in [-128, 127]^16; also property test hadamard roundtrip.
  • vc-quant absorbs the DCT normalization factor — the two crates are coupled by the scaling tables in H.264 sec. 8.5.12.

Violation consequence. Float DCT: platform-dependent rounding; encoder on x86 AVX builds different reference frame than decoder on aarch64; I1 violated on first P-frame; two "conformant" decoders produce different output bytes.

Cross-reference. P1 depends on P3 (integer transforms enable bit-exactness). I3 validates P3 at test time. Phase 3 implements and validates this principle before any other crate uses vc-transform.

P4. Causal Prediction

Statement. Intra prediction reads only already-decoded neighbor samples (top row, left column, top-left, top-right when available). Inter prediction reads only previously decoded frames stored in the DPB at their correct POC via a valid ref_idx.

Mechanism.

  • vc-intra: AvailabilityFlags { top: bool, left: bool, top_left: bool, top_right: bool } computed before any prediction call from MB position + slice boundary information; unavailable samples substituted per H.264 sec. 8.3.1 (nearest available, then DC fallback).
  • vc-inter: ref_idx bounds-checked against ref_list.len() before DPB lookup; out-of-range → Err(DecodeError::InvalidRefIdx); DPB entries are Option<Arc<Frame>>, never raw pointers.
  • Edge-extension for MC: samples outside the frame boundary are replicated from the nearest in-frame sample (pad-by-replication); this is normative per H.264 sec. 8.4.2.

Tradeoff. Prediction at frame/slice edges uses degraded predictors (fewer neighbors → lower-quality DC mode); coding efficiency drops 2–5% near slice boundaries. This is a fundamental H.264 constraint, not a design choice.

Operational implications.

  • vc-intra test suite: all 9 modes × 4 availability configurations (corner, top-row, left-col, interior) — 36 test cases minimum.
  • vc-inter test: ref_idx == ref_list.len() → Err; ref_idx == 0 with empty DPB → Err.
  • Constrained-intra-prediction mode (SPS flag) disables inter-coded neighbor use for intra prediction; vc-intra must check this flag.

Violation consequence. Intra reads uninitialized memory: undefined behavior; security vulnerability when parsing attacker-controlled bitstreams; fuzzer will find this. Inter reads wrong DPB entry: wrong prediction; wrong residual; garbage reconstruction; the error propagates to all future predictions using that frame as reference.

P5. Rate-Distortion Drives Mode Decision

Statement. Every encoding decision minimizes J = D + λ × R where D = distortion (SSE/SSD), R = bits used, λ = 0.85 × 2^((QP−12)/3).

Mechanism.

  • Full RD: for each candidate mode (IntraNxN modes 0–8, Intra16x16 modes 0–3, Inter 16×16/16×8/8×16/8×8, Skip), compute D = SSD(source, reconstructed) after full encode-decode cycle, R = entropy-coded bits; choose argmin J.
  • Fast path (Phase 8, SATD): substitute SATD for SSD in motion search inner loop; estimate R from MV bits and MB type bits only; skip full reconstruction for non-winning modes.
  • Skip mode: if SSD(source, MC_prediction) < threshold, encode as Skip with zero residual; bits = 0 for residual; J evaluated against threshold × λ.

Tradeoff. Full RD: O(modes × partitions × MV-candidates) per macroblock; computationally expensive at naive implementation. Fast path (SATD): 10–50× speedup at <0.5 dB PSNR penalty. This project exposes RdMode { Full, Fast } selectable per-encode.

Operational implications.

  • λ is a f32 computed once per QP level at encode start; the RD comparison J_a < J_b is the only float comparison in the encode path (P1: does not affect reconstruction pixels).
  • vc-encoder logs (poc, mb_addr, winning_mode, J, D, R, qp) per macroblock at RUST_LOG=debug.
  • P10 (observability) surfaces the per-frame mode distribution (intra/inter/skip counts) in FrameStats.

Violation consequence. Fixed-mode encoding (always Intra16x16 DC): 2–4× bitrate penalty at same PSNR; rate control cannot compensate because individual MB decisions are wasteful regardless of QP; B-frame coding benefit is never realized.

P6. In-Loop Filtering Is Inside the Loop

Statement. vc-deblock is applied to every reconstructed frame before storing it in the DPB; this occurs identically in encoder and decoder; deblocking is not a display post-process.

Mechanism. After the last macroblock of a frame is reconstructed, vc_deblock::deblock_frame(&mut frame, &mb_info_grid) filters all 4×4 horizontal and vertical edges in raster order using the boundary-strength algorithm from List et al. (P4). The deblocked frame overwrites the unfiltered frame in the DPB. Both vc-encoder's ReconLoop and vc-decoder call the same vc-deblock::deblock_frame function with identical arguments — the function is stateless, deterministic, and single-source (P9).

Parameters.

  • BS 0: no filtering
  • BS 1–3: weak filter (luma only); thresholds alpha, beta from QP lookup table (H.264 sec. 8.7.2.4 Table 8-16)
  • BS 4: strong filter (DC-mode intra neighbors or across IDR boundary)

Tradeoff. One additional full-frame pass per encoded frame. At 1920×1080 (~8160 MBs), deblocking filters ~32 640 horizontal + 32 640 vertical luma edges per frame, adding ~5–8% encode time. The quality benefit to reference frames typically yields 0.2–0.5 dB PSNR improvement in subsequent frames at typical bitrates, well exceeding the overhead cost.

Operational implications.

  • Deblocking is mandatory in the reconstruction path; there is no skip-deblock flag in normal encode/decode operation.
  • Phase 6 introduces the reconstruction loop without deblocking (noted as TODO, I9 not yet active).
  • Phase 12 integrates vc-deblock into the reconstruction loop; I9 becomes an active assertion from that point.
  • disable_deblocking_filter_idc in the slice header (value 1) disables deblocking for a slice; vc-deblock checks this flag.

Violation consequence. Post-process-only deblocking: encoder uses unfiltered (blocky) reference frames; motion compensation against blocky references produces larger residuals than against filtered references; empirically 10–20% bitrate increase at same PSNR; subjective blocking artifacts visible at low QP in reconstructed P/B frames.

P7. Entropy Coding Is Losslessly Invertible

Statement. CAVLC_decode(CAVLC_encode(s)) == s and CABAC_decode(CABAC_encode(s)) == s for all valid symbol sequences; a single bit error desynchronizes the stream permanently.

Mechanism.

  • CAVLC: deterministic VLC table lookups (coeff_token table indexed by nC, trailing_ones_sign flags, level_prefix/suffix, total_zeros, run_before); encoding and decoding are exact inverses of the same table lookups; no state beyond nC (number of nonzero neighbors).
  • CABAC: binary arithmetic coder with 128 probability states per context; encoder and decoder update each context state via the same StateTransition lookup table after every binary decision; renormalization preserves the interval; bypass coding (for uniform-probability symbols) uses a fixed half-interval split.
  • CabacEncoder and CabacDecoder share a single CabacContext::update(mps_lps: bool) function to guarantee symmetric state evolution.

Tradeoff. No partial recovery from bit errors at the entropy layer; H.264 relies on higher-layer error concealment (FMO, ASO, redundant slices) for resilience. Context reset occurs only at slice boundaries via cabac_init_idc selection.

Operational implications.

  • vc-entropy CAVLC: exhaustive unit test covering all coeff_token codes × total_zeros × run_before table entries (transcribed from H.264 Table 9-5 through 9-11).
  • vc-entropy CABAC: test each binarization scheme (unary, exp-Golomb, fixed-length) encode→decode roundtrip; test arithmetic coder independently of binarization.
  • Integration test: encode a full frame with CAVLC, write bytes to a buffer, decode from that buffer, compare pixels.

Violation consequence. Asymmetric CABAC context update: encoder context diverges from decoder after first symbol; all subsequent MB residuals decode to wrong coefficients; reconstructed frame is noise; PSNR → noise floor (~2–3 dB); no recovery possible within the slice.

P8. Bitstream Conformance

Statement. Emitted NAL units are valid H.264 RBSP with correct emulation-prevention bytes, correct exp-Golomb coding of all syntax elements, and correct slice/macroblock syntax element ordering per H.264 sec. 7.

Mechanism.

  • Emulation prevention: scan RBSP byte-by-byte; insert 0x03 after any 0x00 0x00 XX where XX ∈ {0x00, 0x01, 0x02, 0x03}.
  • Exp-Golomb ue(v): write v+1 as a binary integer; code with k = floor(log2(v+1)) leading zero bits, one 1 bit, then the k least-significant bits of v+1.
  • Exp-Golomb se(v): map v ≥ 0 → 2v, v < 0 → −2v − 1; then apply ue.
  • Syntax order per vc-bitstream: SPS (nal_type=7) → PPS (nal_type=8) → IDR slice (nal_type=5) → P/B slices (nal_type=1); SPS/PPS re-emitted at every IDR boundary.
  • Slice header syntax elements ordered exactly per H.264 Table 7-6; macroblock layer per Table 7-11.

Tradeoff. Strict conformance to H.264 sec. 7 syntax ordering requires maintaining SPS/PPS activation state (which PPS is active, which SPS the PPS references). A non-conformant layout would be rejected by all conformant decoders including FFmpeg and JM. No alternative.

Operational implications.

  • vc-bitstream property test (I7): parse(write(sps)) == sps and parse(write(slice_hdr)) == slice_hdr for exhaustive field combinations.
  • Phase 14 fuzz: 10 000 random byte mutations on valid bitstreams → parser returns Err, never panics, no address-sanitizer findings.
  • External conformance test: FFmpeg can decode vc-encoder output without errors at Phase 6 (I-frame) and Phase 10 (P-frames).

Violation consequence. Missing emulation prevention: decoder start-code scanner finds false 0x000001 in RBSP; misparses slice boundary; frame is corrupt; subsequent frames lost. Wrong exp-Golomb: slice_qp_delta, ref_idx, mv_delta all parse to wrong values; entire slice is undecodable.

P9. Separation of Prediction, Transform, and Coding

Statement. The signal chain has three independent, separately testable stages: (1) prediction → residual, (2) DCT + quantize + dequantize + inverse DCT → reconstructed residual, (3) entropy coding → bitstream. Each stage has well-typed input/output interfaces.

Mechanism.

Stage 1 (vc-intra / vc-inter):
  residual: TransformBlock  =  source_samples − prediction_samples

Stage 2 (vc-transform + vc-quant):
  quant_coeffs: [i32; 16]      =  quant(dct(residual))
  recon_residual: TransformBlock =  inv_dct(dequant(quant_coeffs))

Stage 3 (vc-entropy + vc-bitstream):
  bits                          =  entropy_encode(quant_coeffs, context)

Typed boundaries enforce the separation: TransformBlock between stages 1→2; [i32; 16] coefficients between stages 2→3; BitWriter as the stage 3 output sink. No crate imports the internals of another — only public API.

Tradeoff. Each boundary introduces data copy at block granularity (16–64 i32 values, ~64–256 bytes per MB call). This is negligible (<0.1% of encoding time) relative to the full per-macroblock computation. Testability benefit far exceeds the overhead.

Operational implications.

  • Each crate (vc-intra, vc-inter, vc-transform, vc-quant, vc-entropy) has standalone unit tests that mock adjacent stages with known inputs/outputs.
  • vc-encoder orchestrates stage calls but holds no transform or entropy state itself.
  • Changing from CAVLC to CABAC (Phase 7 → Phase 13) requires only swapping the vc-entropy implementation, not touching vc-transform or vc-quant.

Violation consequence. Fused prediction + transform: a quantization rounding change breaks prediction tests; impossible to isolate the defective stage; adding CABAC requires refactoring prediction logic. Fused transform + entropy: coefficient coefficient scanning order change requires touching DCT butterfly code.

P10. Observability

Statement. Per-frame bits, Y/U/V PSNR, QP, and mode distribution (intra/inter/skip MB counts) are computed and logged after every frame; rate control and RD decisions are explainable from these metrics at any granularity.

Mechanism. vc-core::FrameStats (defined in vc-core, populated by vc-encoder, consumed by vc-cli and rate control):

FrameStats {
    poc: i32, frame_type: FrameType, bits: u64,
    psnr_y: f64, psnr_u: f64, psnr_v: f64,
    avg_qp: f32, min_qp: u8, max_qp: u8,
    intra_mb_count: u32, inter_mb_count: u32, skip_mb_count: u32,
    total_mb_count: u32,
}

vc-encoder emits one FrameStats per frame to a callback / channel. vc-cli psnr subcommand computes PSNR from decoded YUV vs reference YUV independently of the encoder. Rate control in Phase 13 reads bits history from a sliding window of FrameStats.

Tradeoff. PSNR requires one full-frame MSE pass per plane after reconstruction: O(width × height) additions. At 1920×1080, ~2 million additions per frame, ~1–2% encode-time overhead at 30 fps. Unconditionally enabled — no compile-time gate; the cost is low and the benefit of always-on metrics outweighs a theoretical max-throughput mode.

Operational implications.

  • Phase 14: encode source at QP ∈ {10, 20, 30, 40, 51}; collect FrameStats per frame; generate PSNR-vs-bitrate (RD curve); validate I10.
  • vc-cli inspect subcommand: prints per-frame FrameStats as JSON for piping to analysis tools.
  • vc-cli psnr subcommand: reads two raw YUV files, computes per-frame Y/U/V PSNR, prints summary.
  • MB-level observability: RUST_LOG=debug additionally logs winning mode, J, D, R per macroblock.

Violation consequence. No PSNR / bit-count tracking: rate control operates blind; a 3-dB PSNR regression introduced by a quant-table change is invisible until end-to-end subjective review; mode-decision bugs (e.g., always-skip, no inter coding) are undetectable without MB-type distribution data.

P11. GOP Structure Is Declarative, Not Ad Hoc

Statement. The IDR/P/B cadence, reference-frame counts, and reordering behavior are derived entirely from a declared GopConfig/EncodeParams, never decided ad hoc per-frame by scattered conditionals.

Mechanism. GopScheduler { pattern: Vec<FrameType>, period: u32, poc: i32 } computes frame_type, poc, and reference-list membership deterministically from GopConfig { idr_interval, b_cadence, hierarchical_b }; the encoder consults this single schedule everywhere a frame-type or reference decision is needed, and the decoder derives the equivalent structure from parsed SPS/slice headers rather than an out-of-band assumption.

Tradeoff. Declarative GOP scheduling forecloses per-frame adaptive GOP structure (e.g. scene-cut-triggered IDR insertion) unless explicitly modeled as a config mutation — deferred (see Deferred Features Register, scene-cut entry).

Operational implications.

  • GopScheduler is unit-testable in isolation from the rest of the encoder (Phase 11).
  • Changing GOP shape (e.g. IPPP → IBBP) is a config change, not a code change.
  • I12 enforces the DPB-occupancy side of this invariant at runtime.

Violation consequence. Ad hoc reference-list construction scattered through the encoder produces different DPB behavior for encoder vs decoder (I2/I8 violations) whenever a code path forgets to consult the shared schedule.

P12. Fail Closed on Untrusted Input

Statement. Every syntax element parsed from an untrusted bitstream is validated against its normative range before use; on violation the parser returns Err and the decoder discards or conceals the affected unit — it never guesses a "best effort" value and continues.

Mechanism. vc-bitstream and vc-entropy validate every parsed field (QP, mb_type, ref_idx, mv, nal_unit_type) against its legal range at the parse boundary; vc-decoder maps a validation failure to a typed CodecError and a defined concealment/skip action (Threat Model T1–T9), never to a silent clamp-and-continue.

Tradeoff. Strict validation rejects some malformed-but-recoverable streams that a lenient decoder might partially salvage; this project accepts the safety/determinism cost over partial-frame salvage.

Operational implications.

  • Every Phase 14 fuzz target asserts "no panic, Err on invalid" as the pass criterion.
  • Threat Model rows T1–T9 are the enumerated instances of this principle.
  • I11 makes the range-validation contract itself machine-checkable.

Violation consequence. A decoder that clamps out-of-range values and continues silently produces plausible-looking but wrong output on attacker-controlled or corrupted input — undetectable without the fail-closed contract, and a security-boundary violation on untrusted streams.

Principle Cross-Reference

Principle Invariants Enforced Phases Introduced Crates Affected
P1 Bit-exact decoding I1, I2 Phase 3, validated Phase 6+ vc-transform, vc-intra, vc-inter, vc-deblock
P2 Drift-free loop I2 Phase 6 (I-frame), Phase 10 (P), Phase 11 (B) vc-encoder ReconLoop, vc-quant, vc-transform
P3 Integer transforms I1, I3 Phase 3 vc-transform
P4 Causal prediction I4 Phase 5 (intra), Phase 9 (inter) vc-intra, vc-inter
P5 RD mode decision I10 (indirectly) Phase 13 vc-encoder
P6 In-loop filtering I2, I9 Phase 12 vc-deblock
P7 Entropy invertible I6 Phase 7 (CAVLC), Phase 13 (CABAC) vc-entropy
P8 Bitstream conformance I7 Phase 2 vc-bitstream
P9 Stage separation All (testability) All phases All crates
P10 Observability I10 Phase 6 (basic PSNR), Phase 14 (full) vc-core FrameStats, vc-encoder, vc-cli
P11 Declarative GOP structure I8, I12 Phase 11 vc-encoder GopScheduler, vc-inter Dpb
P12 Fail closed on untrusted input I7, I11 Phase 2, hardened Phase 14 vc-bitstream, vc-entropy, vc-decoder

7. System Invariants

The table below expands each invariant (I1–I10) with enforcement location, detection method, telemetry, recovery, and debug approach. Every invariant must be machine-checkable in at least one test; invariants marked "code defect" have no runtime recovery — a failing test is the only signal.

ID Invariant Statement Why It Matters Enforced In Violation Detection Telemetry Recovery Debug Methodology
I1 A bitstream decodes to unique, deterministic output pixels on any conformant decoder Interoperability requires identical reconstruction across platforms; I2 depends on I1 vc-transform, vc-intra, vc-inter, vc-deblock (all integer arithmetic — no float in reconstruction paths) CI: decode same bitstream on x86-64 + aarch64; sha256(output.yuv) must match; nightly cross-platform CI job FrameStats.psnr_y divergence between runs; per-frame SHA-256 in debug log Re-encode from nearest IDR; file bug with reproducible bitstream + platform info diff <(decoder_a bitstream) <(decoder_b bitstream) byte-by-byte; bisect to first diverging MB; identify first non-integer operation

I11 Every parsed syntax element value lies within its H.264-normative range before use in reconstruction (qp ∈ [0,51], mb_type a defined enum variant, ref_idx < ref_list.len(), mv within the level-derived range) Enforces P12 (fail closed); an out-of-range value used silently is undefined reconstruction behavior and a security-boundary violation on untrusted input vc-bitstream parse-layer field validators (SliceHeader, MacroblockHeader), vc-decoder dispatch Fuzz target asserts every out-of-range input returns Err, never a panic or silent clamp; unit tests table-drive all boundary values (qp=51/52, mb_type max+1) parser.range_violation_count per field type, logged with NAL offset Reject the NAL/slice, emit Err(...), apply Phase 14 concealment path Replay the fuzz corpus crash file; identify which field validator was missing or off-by-one
I12 The DPB never holds more frames than SPS.max_ref_frames, and no scheduled B-frame reference resolves outside the currently valid ref_list_l0/ref_list_l1 An over-full DPB signals a marking/eviction bug (T3-adjacent); a B-frame referencing outside its ref lists is an I8 violation waiting to happen structurally vc-inter Dpb::insert / evict_unused, vc-encoder GopScheduler (P11) Assert dpb.frames.len() <= max_ref_frames after every insert; property test scripted GOP patterns against the scheduler dpb.occupancy gauge crossing DPB_MAX − 1 Force sliding-window eviction; if still over capacity, treat as a code defect (no runtime recovery) Dump DPB occupancy time series across the offending GOP; compare against expected schedule from GopConfig
